551. JOHN CAMPBELL and JOHN WATSON were indicated for stealing, on the 1st February, 1 handkerchief, value 2 s. the goods of James Watson, from his person.
JAMES WATSON . I live at No 15, Windmill-street, Tottenham-court-road. I was in Drury-lane on the 1st of February, about five o'clock—in consequence of information from somebody, I felt my coat-pocket, and missed my pocket-handkerchief—I went to the office at seven o'clock it was produced to me—this is it.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. Were there a number of persons moving about, I do not know which—there is no mark on it—the mark is picked out—there was a mark on the one I lost—it was the same colour and pattern as this.
Q. Will you swear this is not your handkerchief? ( producing another.) A. No, it is not, mine was marked with white—my initials are J. w., and the initials on this one are J. W.—it is exactly the same pattern and colour as that—it is not nine by the mark.
JOSHUA COLLISS . I am a glass-cutter. I was in Drury-lane on this evening—I saw the two prisoner in company with another, and saw them walk behind Mr. Watson—Campbell took a handkerchief from the prosecutor's pocket, which appeared to be a Yellow one, and handed it to Watson, and they all ran away; there were three in company—I am certain as to Campball taking the handkerchief—I pursued him—there was a person named Neale in company with me—I sent him after Mr. Watson, to ask if he had lost any thing—he said he had lost a handkerchief—I parsued, and took Campbell up a turning in Drury-lane—I brought him down to Drury-lane, intending to take him to the station, as I could not see a constable—he said, "Do not take me to Bow-street, take me to Hatton-garden"—I took him to the nearest, which was Bow-street—I went up Drury-lane afterwards, and saw the prisoner, Watson, with nine or ten more—I got a police-constable and brought him there, and we saw Watson with a handkerchief in his hand, which the policeman took from him—it appeared to be the same that Campbell received from Watson, but there are many of the same sort.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you mean to swear positively that that is the man who had the handkerchief? A. Yes, he had the one that the policeman took from him—I swear that I saw Campbell give the handkerchief into the hands of Watson—I cannot say whether Watson had the handkerchief that came out of the prosecutor's pocket when he was taken—I saw one taken from Watson by the policeman—I swear the one that came out of the prosecutor's pocket was given to Watson, and he took it—I was about five or six yards from them—I did not instantly raise the alarm—I pursued and took Campbell—my attention was more particularly attracted to him—I am certain Watson is the person to whom Campbell gave the handkerchief—I pointed him out—there was nothing to prevent his walking away—he had some sausages in the handkerchief—there were person in carts passing at the very time of this robbery, and I was on the opposite side of the way—I am working on my own account, and live at No. 39,
Clerkenwell-green—I have never been in any difficulty—I was charged once with an assault on an informer, but never with felony or misdemeanour—I have appeared as a witness—I was never in custody more then once that I recollect—I was once in company with a person who broker a window, who had tendered a bad stilling—I was locked up, and discharged by the magistrate—I was in company with him and drinking some liquor in a public-house—I was charged with being in company with him and—breaking the window which I paid for at the office, he was no friend or acquaintance of mine—I met him casually.
Q. How came you to swear that you had only once been in custody, and that for an assault? A. I did not recollect it at first—I did not forget it—I will swear that was the only time I was before a Justice—I do not recollect any other charged—I cannot say whether I was up for another charged—I will not swear I was not.
COURT. Q. Were you ever charged with any offence? A. I was but I was never tried in any court of justice—I brought some goods of a person, and was taken up, but I paid for them at the office.
MR. DOANE. Q. Had you forgotten that? A. No. I did not wilfully keep it beck—I have not forgotten going to Mr. Hibbert's—I did not say I would drop the charged if he would give me some money—he offered me 2 l. and I refused it and the officer(C130) knows it—I was told I was wanted at No. 112. Drury-lane—I went there—Mr. Hibbert is a butcher, I believe, but the shop was closed—there is a butcher's shop next door—I saw nothing to make me think it was a butcher's, excepting his dress—I did not make any offer about money, it was offered, and I refused—I went to Mr. Neale to know if he would go with me, and we went in company—Neale made the offer and I not heard—I was there all the while—he might have made the offer and I not hear, because he spoke to Mr. Hibbert after we bad been to have something to drink—we went to the public-house after the money had been offered, and I refused it.
COURT. Q. You say you went in consequence of some message? A. Yes, a lad brought a message, and I went to Neale to ask him to accompany me to No. 112, Drury-lance—the offer was first made in this court, on Saturday, by Mr. Hibbert, and I went to Neale's Yesterday morning—Mr. Hibbert said, "what case are you in here?"—I said, "Campbell and Watson"—he said, "That is the very case I am here on, come outside, and have something to drink"—he said, "This can all be settled for a little money"—I said, "I am bound in a bond of 40 l. for this"—I would not listen to it.
Mr. Doane Q. Then the first of this took place in this Court? A. Yes' and the first I heard from Hibbert was about this offer—I had not then ben to his shop—he asked us to come Yesterday—I said, "I do not think I can"—he give us his name. and I think he told us what he was.
Q. Did you not swear that what induced you to go to Hibbert's was a message brought by a lad for you, to go to No. 112, Drury-lane, and you did not know what it was about? A. I bad a massage besides—I did not know what he wanted me for—I refused any bribe from him—bribery had been talked of, but I had no conception what he wanted me for—the message came to me yesterday morning, while I was at breakfast. Joseph Neale. I am a straw-hat presser and manufacturer, I live at No 15. vine-street, Hatton-garden I have lived there two years—I was in Drury-lane on the night of the 1st of February, about live o'clock—s young man named Colliss was with me—I observed, the prisoner Campbell
put his hand into the prosecutor's pocket, take out a handkerchief and hand it to Watson—there was another with them—Colliss ran down a turning after Campbell—he told me to go and tell the man that that boy had picked his pocket, and then said, "No, go and ask him if he has lost any thing"—I did, and he said he had—I told him to follow me we went down the turning, and Colliss took Campbell—the other two ran away—I am sure the prisoner Watson is the one who received the handkerchief from the other—Colliss took Campbell to the office, after grate resistance—in coming up Drury-lane again, we saw Watson with a handkerchief in his hand, in company with some others—I am sure he is the same who received the handkerchief, but I must give him credit, he said he was very Willing to go any where, and did go quietly—one of the boys said the handkerchief which Watson had in his hand was his, and Watson was going to give to give it to another boy to take home with some sausages—the handkerchief had no mark when I saw it—when I was standing in Court the other day, a man in a butcher's dress came and offered to give me 5 l., if I could get the prosecutor out of the way, and keep away myself—I said. "No, certainly not"—I should know the man if I were to see him, his name is Hibbert—for the last two days he has been trying to make us intoxicated—he is here now—this is the man—when I went over the way to get a glass of ale. he was there, dodging me shout—I drank with him yesterday at the bottom of Drury-lane—I met him in Drury-lane—I was fetched to go there by Colliss, who called on me—he said a person wanted me, that he suspected it was the same man, and he would go and see what he wanted—I did not go to his house Colliss did—I have ascertained that it is his house—I was on the other side of the way—Colliss did not go in—he was not away from me five minutes—when I saw him again Hibbert was with him, and then they beckoned me—I would not go to them, they came to me, and we went to a public-house—I said I should have nothing to drink, or if I had, I should pay my part—I put 2 d. and gave it to this man—he had 6 d. worth of rum, and called in two or three others directly—I met Shackle a Bow-street officer, and told him what Hibbert had said—he said, "Mind what you are about, do nothing of the kind, keep yourself harmless. "
Cross-examined. Q. Then Hibbert had been trying to corrupt you? A. Yes; he found I was not the person to make any impression on—he came up to Colliss first—I was standing by—that must have been on Thursday or Friday, I think—he has been every day——he never offered more than 5 l. between us—he spoke to Colliss, and named something about some other case—he said, was he in Harris's case?—Collins said, "No, Campbell and Watson's"—I think he meant 2 l. 10 s. a piece for us—he said afterwards that his father would not do what he intended to do, and then he said 2 l.—I did not tell the policeman this—I told him I did not want the money—Colliss would not take 2 l. he warted 5 l. this has been talked over here, and several policemen know it—Hibbert did not tell me I should find him at Grury-lane—I know nothing about that—Colliss told me a boy came to his lodgings, and said a person wanted to see him in Drury-lane—the prisoner being taken in Drury-lane, I said, "You may depend upon it, it is some of the prisoners' companions want to molest you"—we went and took a walk in the Park, and came beck that way—we then went and found the number—I walked on the other side of the way—he staid over the way talking to Hibbert—he went up a court—I belive the door is up that way—I went and drank with them, but I paid my share—Colliss left me and
Hibbert together—I was in a hurry to go home—I walked ontside the door—I said, "I cannot stop here, I must be home to dinner"—Hibbert followed me out into the street—he said, "Will you have it?" and put his hand into his pocket—I am a straw-hat manufacturer—I have a machine of my own—my wife has woman to manufacturer—I press them—I have the first floor back and front room—I have been there two years, and in the business eight years—I never carried on any other business—I was a wind-instrument maker before—I have always been carrying on my business—I was taken up once, a long while ago, on a mere trifle—I was once at Hatton-garden on suspicion of a boy that stole some things—the boy came backwards and forwards to my place—they did not take me up—it was requested that I should go—I slept at home the same night—they never took me for any things else—I was never taken for passing bed money—I was once in custody for debt, never for any things else—it was not a debt under false pretences—I never was in the County jail—I have been in Newgate, but not of my own accord—I will explain in to you—Mr. Sergeant Arabin said the witnesses ought to have been in the place where I was—I was coming from my own door—there was a cry of "Stop thief"—the man had robbed the shop—I stopped him, and took him—he turned round and said I had robbing the party instead of him—I was charged with felony—I was convicted, and was three weeks in the House of Correction.
JURY. We cannot believe either of these witnesses.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18360201-552]
552. JOHN SIMPSON . was indicted for stealing, on the 3rd January, 1 parasol, value 1 s. 6 d.; and 2 necklaces, value 7 s.; the goods of Catherine Mulvaney, the younger.
CATHERINE MULVANEY, JUN . I am the daughter of Catherine Mulvaney. I had been living at Wapping—I am 18 years of age—the articles stated were given me as a present—I lived with Mr. Kaye formerly—the prisoner lodged there also—I missed a parasol, and one of the necklaces—the other has not been found.
Cross-examined. MR. PAYNE. Q. What is this house of Mr. Kaye's? A. A public-house, where sailors lodge—I was servant of all work—I had seen this parasol last Sunday five weeks, and afterward found it at my mother's house—there was another necklace of white cornclian—I never wore this—nor the other—nor used the parasol—they were given me more than four weeks before I missed then—they were in a drawer, in the best bed-room where captains slept—drawer was not locked—my gowns were there—I saw them on the Sunday, and missed them on the Monday—there were two beds there—I had a box of my own, but it was not there—my mistress allowed me to keep my things in that drawer—I saw them every time I went to the drawer—the string of these beads broke, and I tied them in the middle—the parasol has a darn in it—there is no other mark by which I know them—the prisoner I believe is a ship-carpenter—he had been living in the house for some time—about four or five weeks—when he came on shore he used to board and sleep there—I found these things at my mother's house on the 29th, a fortnight and two days after I missed them.
Catherine Mulvaney. On the 17th, I think, the prisoner came with a bundle to my house—I had seen him on the Saturday night before—he had asked my husband to go with him and buy a pail—he brought the
bundle to take on board one of the Yorkshire traders to Whitby, directed to his wife—my husband is a waterman—he told my husband he would came on Tuesday, and bring another bundle, and not to part with that until he brought the other—my daughter was cleaning, and took up the bundle and felt the beads—she said most likely they are Miss Kaye's—I then went to Mr. Fox—he directed me to go get an officer, and have the bundle searched—these thing were founded by Mr. Sylvester in the bundle, with two white shirts wrapped round them.
Cross-examined. What is your daughter's name who lived at Mr. Kaye's? A. Kitty; she told me she had a parasol—I saw her sue it, nor the beads—she did not bring these things to me—she only told me she had got them, and then she said they were lost—the bundle was tied up with a cord, and sewn with some black thread—it was brought there on Sunday—the prisoner was to come again on Tuesday, but he came on Wednesday night, and was taken.
BOYD SYLVESTER . I am an officer. I was called in, and found this parasol and beads in the bundle—the prosecutrix described the heads by the two knots, and the darn on the top of the parasol, before I opened it.
JOHN NICHOLS ( Police-constable K 88). I took the prisoner—he said the parasol and beads were his, he had brought them from Bomday.
Cross-examined. Q. When did he say this? A. While I was taking him from Mrs. Mulvaney's to the station-house, about eight o'clock in the evening, on Wednesday.
Prisoner I did not tightly apprehend what I said when I went before the Magistrate—I took the parasol and the beads, but I did not exactly know what I was doing at the time—I had been on the spree for a little time, and was a little groggy.
NOT GUILTY .

SAMUEL LEVY . I am a salesman, and live in Great James-street, Lisson-grove,
about half an hour's walk from the prosecutors's—I bought this hat of the prisoner for 9 d —I asked who it belonged to—she said her son—I said, "Where is your son?" she said he was gone out porting—I said, "Why do you sell it?"—she said, "I have had nothing to cat for the last two days"—she seemed rather distressed.
Prisoner I was in liquor all night—I do not remember taking the hat—I remember selling it, as had nothing to give the children the next morning for breakfast.

JOHN ROLLS . I am in the service of Mr. Bassett, a pawnbroker, of Great Queen-street. I took in this silk for 10 s., I belive of the prisoner—he said he brought it from his mother, and was fifteen years of age.
WILLIAM ROBERTS . I am shopman to Messrs. Walmsley, pawnbrokers, in Drury-lane. I have some silk pawned for 12 s., and a gown for 6 s., by the prisoner—he said his mother sent him with it—his mother is in the habit of pledging at out shop—she things by her servant.
JAMES SCOTT . I am shopman to Most and Appleton, of Little Warner-street, Clerkenwell. I produce a brown silk gown, which I took in of the prisoner—I lent 18 d. on it—it is worth about half-a-crown—he said he was fifteen years of age, and came from his mother—he gave the name of Keefe.
Prisoner I did not pawn, it, another boy did, who was not half my size. Witness I believe it to be the prisoner.
Prisoner Mr. Harris told my mother, if I would confess every thing, he would try to do something to get me employment.
BARNETT HARRIS re-examined I did not—I never saw him from the time I missed them till he was at the station; nor did I see his mother—she occupies my third floor, and is very industrious.
---- LATHAM. I am the prisoner's mother. Mr. Harris told me that he suspected him, and nobody else; and if he would acknowledge where the property was he would do nothing to him—I have a husband who can neither see nor hear—I have never received a farthing of this money.
GUILTY . Aged 14.

556. WILLIAM HOLDING was indicted for stealing, on the 26th of January, 2 waistcoats, value 40 s.; the goods of John Robinson.
JOSEPH MORRIS . The an apprentice to John Robinson, a tailor, of Seymour-street, St. Pancras. The prisoner came in on the 26th of January, at a quarter before eight o'clock for a penny-worth of silk—I told him we did not sell trimmings; but they did over the way, at the haberdasher's—he said, "I thought you sold silk, seeing it hanging up there, two needle fulls would do: and then he said thread would do, it was to mend his father's coat—he pressed me so long. I let him have a skein for 1 d., the threw
down 1 s —I went into the parlour for change—a witness said she would go for change—the prisoner said, "I will go"—the witness said, "No, I will go"—the prisoner held the door in his hand to go, and she insisted upon going herself—when we opened the door, we saw the waistcoat, which the officer has, outside, on the step of the door—it had been on the bar in the window—I had seen it safe about six o'clock—there had only been one other person in the shop, who came to pay a bill—I am sure he did not remove it—I was there when the prisoner first came in—he left the door wide open, and I told him to shut, it, because I heard the rings on the brass bar move, and I thought the wind blew the curtains—when we found the waistcoat, outside, we found there was another gone—we then asked the prisoner where he lived; he said, "No 3, Compton-street, "and he was found to live at No. 1, Compton-place.
ANN FREEMAN . I am the wife of James Freeman. I offered to go for change—the prisoner said, no, he would go—he went to the door and tried to stop me from going out—I got past him; and when I got to the step of the door I picked up the waistcoat, and accused him of taking it—he said it was not him, it was a boy at the door.
Prisoner There are some more boys at the window, and it was one of them who who took it—they said it was a bad shilling—the door was open—when he was serving me he said he heard the window go.
JOHN MORRIS . No one could have taken the one that was at the door—any could have taken the other that was gone.
JOHN GAME ( Police-constable S 86) I took the prisoner—he told me on the road the station-house, that it did not matter so longer as they did not see him take it; his father and mother were dead, and he wanted the silk to mend his own coat.
GUILTY . Aged 16— Confined One Month the first and last week to be solitary.

DAVID HALEY . I live at Highgate. I saw the prisoner go into Mr. Attwood's yard and come out again, but I cannot tell what he took—he had a bag, but no basket.
MARY ATTWOOD . This is my gown—I laid it out to dry on the line in the yard—my husband came to me, and I missed it.
Prisoner's Defence I was out with crockery—a man said, would I buy a gown; I gave him a very handsome set of jugs for it—I had two or three more houses to got to, and this man came and took me.

563. THOMAS CASEY was indicted for a misdemeanor.
MESSRS. BULLOCK and GURNEY conducted the Prosecution
WALLER WITNERS . I shall be twelve years old next birth-day, and live with my father, in Noble-street, City. The defendant keeps a coolshed in Monkwell-street—I have been in the habit of buying coals there—on the last Friday in January, I went there to buy some coals—I found Mr. Casey and a boy there—I told him my father wanted half a hundred weight of coals—I saw him put them into the scales—before he did that he put a 7lb. weight on the other side against the wall—I them went home, and told my father—I went back again, and told Mr. Casey to send the coals home with me, and they were sent directly—I went with them to my father's.
COURT. Q. Did you go with the coals all the coals way from Casey's house, to your father's? A. Yes—nobody interfered or took any out—that I swear.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. This was in the evening, was it not? A. Yes—I am quite sure that this was in the shop all the time—I hesitated because I had some doubts—I am quite sure he is the man—I am not sure that he is the man that was there.
MR. GURNEY. Q. Was the person that was there dressed differently to this man? A. Yes; he is cleaner. I never saw the gentleman in such clothes before.
COURT. Q. Had your father dealt there long for colas? A. Yes—the man did not always serve me—the boy did sometimes—I do not know him in those clothes.
MR. DOANE. Q. You saw the person who served you put a weight into the scale? A. Yes; he deliberately took in off he floor, so that I could see very plainly—I did not say thing to him, or cry out—I did not like—the weight was there when I went back—when the coals were taken, a person named Sharp was there—he took he weight off—he was not there when I first went in—he came in afterwards.
THOMAS WITHERS . I am the witness's father, and have been in the habit of having coals of the prisoner. On the 29th of January, my son came back about the coals he afterwards went back to Casey—the prisoner is the person who keeps the coal-shed—I saw the coals with when my son came with them—I asked the boy whether he and brought me good weight, and told him to put them on the floor in the bag—I then took hold of the bag. I took them to Mr. Haine's, in Noble-Steet, and saw them weighed—there they were 5lbs. short of weight, besides the bag—I went directly to Casey's shop—I saw him, and said, "Halloo, Governor, I am doubtful whether you sent my right weight of coals"—his boy had brought the coals back with me—the prisoner made an answer to that—a person named
Sharp, asked for a light to look at the Scales—I examined the scales, and found behind the scale in which the coals were put, next to the wall, on the swivel, 1 7lb. weight behind the scoop—I pointed that out to Casey, and asked him what that did here—He said he not know, he knew nothing about it, he had not put it there—I told him I thought it was a great imposition, I though he had been robbing me nicely.
Cross-examined. Q. He said he he knew nothing about it? A. Yes—the weight was not fixed, it was temporarily hooked on, hanging outside the scoop next the wall—Sharp was hear me before I saw it—he took a candle and looked behind, or he could not have seen the weight—I can swear Sharp did not put the weight there.
THOMAS HENRY HAINES . I am a cheesemonger, and live at No. 19, Noble-street. Mr. Withers came to me, a man and a boy accompanied him with a bag of coals—they weighed, to the best of my recollection, 49lbs. which is 7lbs. short of the half cwt., allowing for the sack.
MR. DOANE addressed the Jury on behalf of the defendant.
GUILTY . Aged 31— Fined £10, and Confined Six Weeds.
Before Mr. Recorder
[trialAccount t18360201-564]
564. WILLIAM ROTHERHAM was indicted for that he, having been declared a bankrupt, feloniously did conceal part of his personal estate, to the value of 10 l., and upwards.
MESSRS. WIGHTMAN, CLARKSON, GURNEY, and BAILEY, conducted the Prosecution
WILLIAM FOR . I am a warehouseman. I have known the prisoner from seven to nine years—he has carried on the business of a linen-draper, in Shoreditch.
Cross-examined by MR. BODKIN. Q. Did you know him intimately? A. Nothing his transactions in business—he kept, I believe as many as thirty of forty persons in his establishment.
GEORGE WARD . I am clerk to Ellis and Everington, and have been se about nine years. Mr. Everinton jointed the firm in August, 1834—I have known the prisoner four or five years—he was in the habit of dealing with out firm—some of these bills ( produced) are drawn by Wynne Ellis alone, and the others by Ellis and Everington—they are accepted by William, Rotherham, the prisoner—I think I can speak to his hand-writing—I believe these to be is hand-writing—I have seen him accept bills.
Cross-examined by MR. BODKIN. Q. Do you mean you saw him acaccept some of these bills? A. I cannot say positively that the accepted all these, but some of these are my writing—it has been usual for me to draw them, at his house, and he has accepted them—I have often seen write—I believe they are his hand-writing.
(The bills produce by the witness were as follows:—one for 68 l. 15 s., one for 203 l. 19 s., one for 247 l. 18 s., and one 50 l. 9 s. 6 d.) MR. BODKIN. Q. To what extent has the prisoner dealt at your house in the last few years? A. I do not know—I cannot swear that it has not been to the amount of 4, 000 l
WM. GLAZEBROOK DRESSER . I am collecting-clerk to Messrs. Leaf. Cole Son, and Co. warehouseman, in Old Change. The prisoner was indebted to them in the beginning of October last—in consequence of some instructions from my employers, I called on him in Shoreditch, on the 4th of October.
1834—I saw one of the clerks—I told him I called for our account, from Leaf and Co.—I do not think I mentioned the sum—he left the counting-house, and went out at a back, door, through a door at the end of the counting-house—I cannot say exactly what time it was—I should think about one o'clock—I do not know where that door led to—he stayed out about ten minutes—I received an answer a person—I do not recollect whether it was the same, that "Mr. Rotherham is out, but we will send a cheque on"—I went away—I went there on the day preceding for the same purpose, and applied in a similar way—I do not recollect the answer I got—I did not get the account.
Cross-examined. MR. BODKIN. Q. You delivered the message to a clerk in the counting-house, when do not know? A. Yes, and on the Tuesday after, I believe, a portion of the account I called for was paid—he paid 130 l., part of that accounts, on the 9th October—within a week, or a few weeks, he paid the rest—the whole amount was 150 l. 14 s. 3 d —I believe Leaf and Co. did not afterwards supply him with goods on credit—I cannot tell whether they supplied him on cash, because they would not then came into the ledger.
FREDERICK COLLIER . I was a clerk at the at the prisoner's and was so in the latter part of 1834. I remember Mr. Dresser calling at the bankrupt's several times, about the latter end of September, and the beginning of October, 1834. I remember his coming on the 4th of October for a settlement of an account which I believe was then due—he told me he came for a settlement of the account—I said I did not think Mr. Scafe was in the way—he then asked me whether he could see Mr. Rotherham—I told him I would go and see—I went up stairs, and saw Mr. Joseph Rotherham and Mr. William Rotherham together, in the parlour—I told Mr. William Rotherham that Mr. Dresser, the clerk from Leafs' was waiting below, and wished to see him—he told Joseph Rotherham to go down and speak to hi—I believe Joseph Rotherham left the room first—that was all William said, that I remember—Joseph Rotherham went into the counting-house, though I did not go in with him—I went into the back warehouse, and suppose Joseph Rotherham spoke to Dresser—I saw his lips moving, though I could not hear him.
JOSEPH BENFOLD . In November last. I was assistant to Mr. Willis, the officer of the Sheriff of Middlesex. I went to the prisoner's house on the 15th of November, in the afternoon—Mr. Wm, Willis, jun was with me—we went to arrest the prisoner—we went into the shop in Shoreditch, and asked for Mr. William Rotherham—Willis waited outside, till I had been in a necessary time for him to come in—I asked if Mr. William Rotherham was at home—the first person I saw was his brother—I cannot recollect his Christain name, but think I should know the person if I were to see him—there were several other in the shop—I said, "I have a letter for Mr. William Rotherham, "and asked to see him—he said he was not home—that was before I told him I had a note for him—I told him that it was of particular importance that I wanted to see him—he then said, if I would give him the letter, he would go and see—I gave him the note—he then went immediately out into a passage, and up stairs; and I followed him—he did not ask me to follow him—I saw him give the letter to his brother, the prisoner, in the room up stairs—I was outside at the time, but I saw into the room—I followed into the room, and saw the prisoner break the letter and read it—there was some other person in the room, but I cannot recollect who it was—his brother went of the room, when I
went in—the wanted to know what business it was—he said he knew nothing about it—I said, "The fact is, I have called from Wynne Ellis respecting his account"—he then said it was not him, it was his brother—I told him that I had a writ against him—he said it was not him, it was his brother (his brother was not there at the time)—I then said he must have great assurance to take and open his brother's letter—I can scarce recollect whether his brother came back, but I think he did—I told the prisoner he must go along with me, I should not leave him—he said, "Take me at your peril"—I told him I should do so, and he repeated the words over or three times—I died take him—he was in his shirt sleeves—he had no coat on—he called for his coat and boots—after he had been there some minutes he attempted to go out of the room—I told him be should not go, unless I went with him—he did go, and I followed him—he seemed very angry because I followed him—he put his coat and boots on—I took him into the street, and the officer was sitting in the shop—the prisoner came out at the side door into the street—I had not hold of him then—I had touched him before—Mr. Willis saw me pass the door, and he came out of the shop, and jointed me—I said to Mr. Willis, "Mr. Rotherham denies his name—he says his name is not William, he is not the person we want"—Mr. Willis asked him what his name was—he said, "I am not the person you want—my name is not William Rotherham, and at your peril touch not—Willis said, "Tell me what your name is"—he said he would not—Willis then said to me, "Go and fetch one of the shopmen out"—I went and told one of the shopman that Mr. William Rotherham wised to speak to him immediately—the shopman came out and asked me where he was—I said, "He is close by, I believe—that is Mr. William Rotherham, is it not?—he said, "Yes"—I said, "He wishes to speak to you"—the shopman the made towards him; and when he got close to him, William Rotherham said, "That will do, I shall not want you now"—he did not make any other remark at that time—we then brought him away to the lock-up-house, in Cursitor-street, Chancery-lane.
Cross-examined by MR. LEE. Q. I believe the debt was paid that same night? A. I cannot tell that, he was discharged, I believe that same evening—I cannot whether he was discharged—I did not see him—I did not know it till I was told of it the next day—I was not there the next day, to my knowledge—I cannot say whether I was or not—not to my knowledge—I heard he went of the next day—I live in St. John's-square—I have got my living for he last three years by going with the Sheriff's officers—that is the only way—I am married—my wife is alive—I have no family—I shall not answer the question whether I am living with my wife—I do not think it is a legal question—I think it is not proper to answer it, it is bringing family affairs into publicity.
Q. Have you ever been taken up, or charged with bigamy? A. No, never I have never been taken to any office, or charged with any thing—I have never been charged with bigamy—when I called, I saw the prisoner's brother—I do not know his christian name—he told me he was his brother—Mr. Willis gave me the note—it came from our office—it was sealed—I might have written it, but I cannot tell—it was given to Joseph—it is not here, nor a copy of it—the first I saw was the prisoner's brother—after the prisoner had got it, and read it, he said he was not the person; it was directed Mr. William Rotherham—and upon the that he put on his boots and coat. and was about the room, and I said he was my prisoner,
and I should take him—those were my expression—I might have Said more, but I do not recollect that I did after he had read the note I produced a writ, and gave him a copy of it—I have not got it now—we do not keep them.
MR. WIGHTMAN. Q. Was that a real letter or a sham letter? A. It was a sham letter, purposely for an introduction, to get an interview with Mr. William Rotherham, and his name was in full on the letter—he was the person we want to arrest—the prisoner is the man.
WILLIAM WILLIS, JUN . I am the officer to the sheriff of Middlesex. I went with the last witness to arrest Mr. William Rotherham, on the 15th of November—I went to the shop after Benfold had been in some time—he took a letter with him—I went into the shop and inquired if Mr. William Rotherham was at home—the young man said he did not know—while I was there I saw Mr. William Rotherham and Benfold pass the door in the street—I walked out after them, and saw them together—Benfold said, "Never mind; I am not the person you want, and at your peril take me"—he was then going to walk away—I said, "No, stop a moment; I must know who you are before I take you, if your name is not William Rotherham"—I told Benfold to go into the shop and tell one of the young men that Mr. William Rotherham wished to speak with him outside—I said that in the presence of William Rotherham—we were all three standing together—Benfold went into the shop and fetched out a young man—when he came out he came towards the prisoner, and the prisoner said, "That will do, "I think he said Edward; "I am the man you want"—he said to the young man, "You may go back;" "I am the man you want"—I then took him to the lock-up-house.
Cross-examined by MR. BODKIN. Q. What lock-up-house did you take him to? A. Slowman's in Cursitor-street-we generally take them there if they wish it—he wished to go there, because Mr. Slowman would give an undertaking for him—Benfold was in the habit of going there with me sometimes, and by himself sometimes—I believe he did not go with me there the next day—I did not go myself—I herd that the prisoner was discharged the next day—that was not through Benfold going to make inquiries—I heard it from my brother—I did not write this letter—it might be Benfold or my father—I could tell if I saw it—it was sealed up in the usual way—he denied that he was the person we wanted, and said his name was not William Rotherham—I then went into the shop—not a word was said about Leaf's house in my presence—I can give you a notion of the contents of the letter.
THOMAS EDWARD STUBBS . I am a officer of the Court of Bankruptcy I produce the fiat issued against William Rotherham, and likewise the adjudication—it appears upon the fiat that it is entered up on record, and the fiat is signed, "John Thomas Church".
(It appeared by the proceedings that several meeting had been held by adjournments, on the 9th and 23rd of January, 2th of February, 31st of March, 9th of April, 7th and 26th of May, 2nd and 19th of June, and the 2nd and 9th of July, the prisoner having made the usual oath that he had made a full disclosure of his estate and effects.)
Cross-examined by MR. BODKIN. Q. Did you attend all the meetings? A. No; all but his first surrender, and the paper signed by Mr. Meredith—the prisoner all through protested against his commission, and I have heard that he took proceedings to try the validity of it—I have seen Mr.
Asharst attend with him, as his attorney—I have heard it has been tried at Westminster.
MR. WIGHTMAN. Q. What became of that action? A. I have heard the bankrupt was nonsuited.
HENDERSON SCAFE . I went into the prisoner's service as clerk on the 4th of December, before the bankruptcy—that was in December, 1834—among other employment, I was in the habits of going to the Bank of England for the purpose of taking the monies taken in the shop to be exchanged for notes—I took monies to the bankers occasionally for him—I remember, on the Monday previous to the bankruptcy, the prisoner giving me cheques and cash to the amount of about 250 l.—on the Saturday I had taken to the London and Westminster bank about 215 l. in small notes and cash—my instructions were to get them exchanged for large notes.
Q. Was the transaction on the Monday at the Bank of England? A. yes; he gave me small notes and cheques to the amount of 250 l —I got partly money and partly notes for the cheques—I then proceeded to the Bank of England with the whole, and got large notes—the largest note I received was a £200 note—I had not put anything on the notes I brought from the bankrupt, and from the bankers in Lombard-street, till I got to the Bank, when I wrote "William Rotherham" on the top of the notes—(looking at some notes) here is the name on every one of these—I received a £200 note, and delivered it to the bankrupt—I do not remember what amount of cash I had in gold and silver, but I received a ticket like this (looking at one)for the gold—that was given under the gallery—I never received a £200 note for the bankrupt except on those occasions, on the Saturday before, when I took some money to the London and Westminster Bank, and the £200 note which I got at the Bank of England.
Cross-examined by MR. BODKIN. Q. I see you wrote the name on the top; was it always your customs to do that with the notes you took? A. Certainly, when I went on similar occasions, which I frequently did.
WILLIAM TAYLOR . I am one of the clerks in the Bank of England. I have the book of entries here—at the date of 5th of January, last year, I find an entry of the payment of a £200 note, No, 1562, dated 18th December, 1834, paid to the name of Rotherham—I received twelve notes, amounting to 115 l. and a ticket from the tellers of 85 l. paid in cash—this is the cash ticket, and twelve notes which amount to 115 l.—these I delivered a £200 note of the number and date I mentioned—I posted the note, and William Higham paid out the note—the pay-clerk calls the number and date of the note.
Cross-examined by MR. LEE. Q. You do not speak as to the number and date of the notes you have before you? A. No; the pay-clerk is liable to make a mistake certainly, I depend upon the sound I hear—Mr. Higham is the person who would pass the note from the box to the party applying—I call the number to him, I had the twelve notes and the teller's ticket—I enter them, asking the individual what we wishes—he replies a £200 note—I turn to my two hundreds, and say, "One 200—62, "which is chequed by my partner, and he gives it from my direction.
Q. But when you refer to four book do you look at the note to see that is corresponds with the number you call out? A. No; I do not—I call out the note next in turn to be used, and in this case it was No. 1562.
COURT. Q. How do you know the number and date you have now stated? A. Our notes are in fifties, and in this case I should call 62—that
would signify 1562—when Mr. Higham delivers the note he does not give me the date—I have a book that shows what notes are to be issued, and I call out the number that comes in order—on this occasions I should say"One 200 is 62"—Mr. Higham would give the note, and I write the name "Rotherham" against it in the book—that does not prove that this is the £200 note delivered.
WILLIAM HIGHAM . I am a clerk in the Bank. On the 5th of January I remember handing a £200 note to a person applying for it—here is my hand-writing on the back of the notes that were given in exchange for it—here are" twelve notes, 115 l. in the name of Rotherham, "Under which I put my initials and this ticket for 85 l. on which my initials are put—I cannot tell the number and date of the £200 note which I gave to the bearer of these twelve notes and this ticket—I gave out the £200 note of the number which the last witness called out to me—I have the note.
CUTHBERT GEORGE BROWNS . I am a clerk in the Bank. On the 21st of August last, I received this £200 notes are paid in we require the person to put his name and address on them—I did not see the person who produced this £200 note to do—I asked his name, he said "Wrightson"—I find the writing of of my partner, William Taylor, on it.
WILLIAM TAYLOR re-examined. I wrote "5, Bethnal-green" on it, it being imperfect I wrote it again, the name of "Robert Wrightson, Esq., No. 5, Bethnal-green," was on it—I did not see him write it, but I presume he did.
CUTHBERT GEORGE BROWN re-examined. Q. Did the person who presented that note receive any others in exchange for it? A. Yes; one £100 note, one £50, £20, and one£10, they are entered in my writing in the cash-book and in the numerical book—I wrote the name of wrightson against the number of the notes we paid—the £100 is No. 10, 557, dated 6th of August, 1835; the £50 is 16, 584, dated 28th of July, 1835; the £20's are 17, 846 and 47 dated 30th of July, 1835; and the £10 note is No. 9500—the name of Wrightson written against them is the name the person gave in—on the third of October last, I received that £100 note again into the bank.
C. G. BROWN re-examined. This is the note produced by Higham—the mane on it is Joseph Rotherham, 39 and 40, Shoreditch. I delivered ten £10n notes, from Nos. 1955, to 1964, dated 24th of August, 1835—I made the entries, and Mr. Taylor delivered the notes—it would be Mr. Taylor's duty to pay these notes.
WILLIAM TAYLOR . I paid the person who brought this £100 note, the ten £10 notes.
WILLIAM HIGHAM . I produce six £10 notes, Nos. 1955, 1956, 1957, 1960, 1963, 1964, which have again come into the bank.
EDWARD MUSGROVE . I am a clerk to my brother, who is an auctioneer, in Austin friars. I knew Mr. George Andrews—I am not aware of what he is—in the month of September, I received from him a sum of 73 l. odd, for some fixtures in a house in Shoreditch—I know the premises formerly occupied by the prisoner—it was for the fixtures of that house—Mr. Andrews had taken these premises of the party of whom Mr. Musgrove was acting—Ellis and Howes—to the best of my recollection, I received more than 73 l., and gave him change—I think it was in notes—I think I received 75 l. I wrote the name of Andrews up on the notes I received.
WILLIAM GIGHAM re-examined. I produce on e£50 note, No. 16, 584,
dated the 28th of July, 1835, one £20, No. 17, 847, dated the 30th of July, 1835, and one £10 note, No. 9500, dated the 21st July, 1835.
MR. MUSGROVE. These are the three notes that I received from Mr. Andrews on that occasion.
GEORGE ANDREWS . I know the prisoner, Mr. William Rotherham—I was acquainted with him four or five years before he came a bankrupt—I knew him carrying on his business in Shoreditch—I was employed to retake those premises for him—it was about four months ago—he asked me to take the house for his brothers Joseph, as he could not get it himself from the landlady—she would not admit Joseph Rotherham as a tenant, and therefore he applied to me to take it for him.
COURT. Q. Did the prisoner want it taken for his brother, and not for himself? A. Yes; and the landlady would not admit his brother.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. In consequence of this, did you apply to Mr. Musgrove? A. Yes; and came to terms—half the amount of the fixtures was to be paid, which was 73 l. or 74 l. to the best of my knowledge—after this I saw Joseph Rotherham, and his brother, the bankrupt was present when the agreement was made—they gave me the money to pay for the fixtures—Joseph gave it me in the presence of William—I think nothing passed respecting the payment of these fixtures, between me and the prisoner and his brother when Joseph gave me the money—they were large notes—the same that I gave to Mr. Musgrove, on the same day—the prisoner made no application to me at any time after this on the subject of letting the house—his brother did.
Cross-examined by MR. BODKIN. Q. Have you seen Joseph Rotherham write? A. Never to my knowledge—he never kept a retail shop in shoreditch, since I have known him.
COURT. Q. Did you go at any time to these premises, after the fixtures were paid for? A. Only three or four times—Joseph was in possession of the premises, and I frequently saw the prisoner there—the place was not finished.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Was William there when you saw Joseph there? A. Yes; I do not know that he was always there—I communicated with Joseph in the presence of William.
COURT. Q. Had you any communication with William, who employed you to negotiate for the house; as to what sources the money would be received from? A. No—I understood from Joseph that it was part of a sum of money that he received with his wife on his marriage.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. When did you last see Joseph? A. In October—he was not married then—I cannot tell whether William was present when Joseph explained the sources from whence he meant to pay for these—I think it was at a house opposite the Excise-office, in Broad-street—I had about ten interviews with them together, and separate.
JAMES OAKSHOT . I live in Shoreditch, and am a carpenter. Joseph Rotherham came to our premises on the subject of some repairs—William Rotherham was not there—In consequence of that application I went to the premises in Shoreditch—I saw Joseph—I was at the time the repairs were done, about the latter end of August or September—when I went to the premises, Nos. 39 and 40, Shoreditch, Joseph was in the shop, and Williams was there—there were some drawings shown me by Joseph, in the presence of my partner—we were to be paid 200 l. for the work done—I was in the habit of seeing the prisoner while the work was going on
upon the premises—I was not in the habit of conversing with him on the subject of repairs—Joseph was the person we made our contract with, but William could not be on the premises without our passing the time of the day with him—we sometimes spoke to William on the subject—I have received two sums of 50 l. each from Joseph, on account of our bill, in notes.
Q. Have these three £10 notes, Nos. 1960, 1963, and 1964 your writing on them? A. No—I have seen notes similar to these before, at the Bankruptcy Court, but I want to be sure before I answer you—I should say this note has been through my hands, by having "Little" on it, which is my partner's name—this is not his writing—he does not write—I write for him—this is not my writing—there is no writing of mine on these notes—this name of "Doggett" on this one, I believe is my writing—I have not the smallest doubt of it—I believe it is my writing—this is No. 1963.
Q. Do you see anything you think to be your handwriting on the other two? A. This one has my writing on it, No. 1934, and so has this one No. 1960—they have all got my writing on them—everyone of them—I did not tell the Jury so at first—I am not in the habit of writing on notes—I did not observe it at first, when I turned the notes over.
Q. Did you complete the work on these premises? A. I did—the whole of our contract was finished—all what we agreed to do—there was another contract—we were paid nothing on the second contract—there was nothing left undone, to my knowledge—I have no note written by the bankrupt in my possession—I had a note from him relative to this transaction—I gave it to Mr. Bowden, the solicitor—I have not seen it since—I gave it to him the day after I was examined at the Bankruptcy Court—nearly the whole of our repairs were fixtures—we have got the whole of the counters and shelves back—that was all the fixtures that were the subject of the contract—I gave nothing for them, because we were not paid—we were paid at first—we received 100 l —I consider we fixed them up for nothing—we were allowed to take them back by Joseph—I saw the prisoner on the subject of these fixtures when he was in the Fleet, about two months back—I went expecting to find Joseph, but I found the prisoner—I found we could not get our money of Joseph, and I asked the prisoner what we were to do—he said he found that Joseph could not open the house, and we had better take our fixtures back again, and we said, "Very well, we would sooner do that than lose the whole"—William said that Joseph wanted to be paid a certain sum of money, and he mentioned 50 l —I said we could not think of doing any such thing, we were losing enough as it was—he said we should give him 20 l —I said we would not do any such thing—he then said I should make him a present of 5 l —nothing else was mentioned between the 20 l. and 5 l.—I forget whether 10 l. was mentioned—it might be—I then took away all the fixtures.
COURT. Q. Then it was by William authority you acted, when you removed them? A. Yes.
Cross-examined by MR. LEE. Q. The contract for these fixtures was entered into between you and Joseph? A. It was during the completion of the first contract William told the men that were working there how to act—how to put up the shelves—he gave directions in the first contract with regards to the size of the shelves, more than once—not to me personally, but in my presence—when I said he never gave me any directions on the first contract, but merely passing the time of day, I meant as to taking the contract—I cannot say whether he interfered more than once—he was there several days when I was not present—he did not give me
directions about the shelves more than once—the direction were given generally, in the superintendence of the work, by Joseph, to me and my partner—I cannot tell on what days I received the first and second 50 l.—I believe the name of "Doggett" on these notes to be my writing.
Q. Up on your solemn oath, will you swear that is your handwriting? now take care? A. I will not swear to it—these are the three "Doggetts" that I said before were my handwriting—I will not swear that this one is my handwriting—I will not swear this other is my writing, it very much resembles my handwriting—I have sworn before that I believe it to be my handwriting, and believe the same now.
COURT. Q. What do you say about these two that you have handed up; have you any doubt about this being your writing on them? A. I do not know that I have.
MR. LEE. Q. How do you spell Doggett? A. Doggett; this one is spelt Doggett, this is Daggett, this other is Doggett.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Do you recollect writing upon the notes that were paid to you for the work, the name of "Doggett"? A. I went to Mr. Doggett's to get change for the noes—not for all three, I believe only one—I very seldom write on notes—I recollect writing the name of "Doggett" on one of them—I have no recollection of writing on the other two.
Q. Now once more, look at the whole three of them carefully; do you believe that the handwriting upon each of these notes is yours? A. I should say it was.
COURT. A. Have you any doubt it is your handwriting? A. No; I have no doubt it is—I recollect writing on one at Mr. Doggett's shop—the last time I saw Joseph Rotherham was in the Fleet, about three months ago.
ROBERT WRIGHTSON . I am father-in-law to Mr. William Rotherham, his wife is my daughter; I live in the house where I was born, on Beth nal-green, and where I have lived for ninety years. I can write—I have been in business many years—this "Robert Wrightson, Esq., 5, Bethnalgreen "is not my writing—I never had this £200 note in my possession on the 21st of August—I have been our of business a length of time—I never had such of money in my possession—I have had 100 l., but I never had this.
Cross-examined by MR. LEE. Q. Can you tell me those handwriting it is? A. No; I do not recollect any one—I never saw Joseph Rotherham write.
FREDERICK COLLIER re-examined. I have seen Joseph Rotherham write—I believe this "Robert Wrightson, Esq." to be his writing.
JOHN MUMFORD . I have a brother of the name of Charles—in the beginning of 1835 my brother was in some difficulties—I was managing his affairs—this bill (looking at one) was never brought to me, but I saw the prisoner on the subject of it—I went to him about it after the bill became due—I think it was about April or May—I asked him where it was—he said he did not exactly know, he could find it out—he did not at that time produce it to me—there was no negotiation between us about paying it—I wished to know where it was—I called several times about it—I proposed through the accountant, who wished me to go, to give 18 l. for it—I offered 18 l. for it, on conditions of its being delivered up—I knew the prisoner was a bankrupt at the time, but I did not know where to apply for it—my brother, who intrusted me to make the application, told me it was an
accommodation bill—I do not know that I told the prisoner it was an accommodation bill, but he knew that, I dare say—I made that offer because we understood it was in a third party' hands, and we thought that be paying 2 s. or 2 s. or 2 d. in the pound we might get it—I paid 18 l.—I don't know when, but it was after the bill was due—I paid the bankrupt 18 l. to take up the bill—I think it was in May or June.
WILLIAM NEWMAN . I am a draper residing at Windsor, and was so previously to 27th December, 1834. I knew the prisoner, William Rotherham—I had seen him once, and done business with him previously to the 27th of December, 1831 he had supplied me with goods—I was not indebted to him toll the 27th of December, when Mr. Newman came to town and purchased goods—I was not with her—she came by my direction—I received some goods about the 29th of December, as far as I can remember—I do not know the prisoner handwriting—I know the acceptance of this bill to be my own handwriting—it was given for the goods—I instructed my wife to purchase of Mr. Rotherham.
FREDERICK COLLIER . The filling up of this bill is Mr. Scafe's handwriting—the signature I believe to be Mr. William Rotherham's.
WILLIAM NEWMAN . I have remitted money to pay this bill to Mr. hay, of Windsor-terrace, City road—the bill became due on 30th March, to the best of my knowledge, and I believe on that day I remitted the money, it being on Sunday—I was well aware it could not be applied for till the 31st—the money was remitted to Mr. Hay where the bill was made payable—I believe the 30th was the Sunday, or else it was 31st—I know it was on Sunday that Mr. Hay ought to have the money to pay it on Monday.
Cross-examined by MR. LEE. Q. These goods were purchased by your wife? A. Yes—she is not here—she is alive and well—I know nothing more than that these goods followed the order which I supposed my wife gave.
MR. LEE to FREDERICK COLLIER. Q. You say you believe the signature to be the handwriting of the prisoner? A. yes, from having seen him write cheques—the body of the bill is Scafe's—he has been accustomed to draw bills for the prisoner—the prisoner was in a very large way of business and was in the habit of signing blank cheques.
EDWARD HAY . I remember, in the month of March, 1835, receiving some money from Mr. Newman, of Windsor—the sum was 35 l. and something—a man came at the expiration of the time with a bill of 35 l. from Rotherham—I paid the man myself—I never saw the prisoner in my life—if this is the bill the lawyer had, I swear I received it—I kept it my self in my iron chest until Newman himself came, and then I delivered it up.
CHARLES BRISTON . I am the clerk to Mr. Belcher, the official assignee under this fiat of bankruptcy—(looking at a book)this is on of the bankrupt's book, which was delivered up under that fiat—here is an entry in it in red ink, which was made by the bankrupt at our office after the fiat—it was produced to him at the court, at the time of the examination—this is one of the books produced when he passed his last examination—here is entered on the credit side of Miss. Scafe's account, "May 16th, 1834, Cash 50 l.; June 2nd, Cash 50 l.; and then, without a date, immediately under the last item, "53 l. 3 s. "
Cross-examined by MR. LEE. Q. Is is not the regular practice to have the books marked by the Commissioners, as having been exhibited before
them? A. It is generally done at the first meeting—there is a regular stamp used by the Commissioners to mark the books which they have examined, and this book does not appear to be so marked—there were a great many books exhibited under this estate—I generally sort out the books to be produced at the meeting, under the direction of some person—there are a great variety of Commissions, and a great number of books—it happens nearly every day in the week—this book was produced at one of the meetings, and the bankrupt had the opportunity of inspecting it—it was one of his principal books—I do not know whether his attention was called to this particular entry—the book was produced at one of the meetings, and the bankrupt had an opportunity of inspecting it scontents—I cannot recollect the amount of debt proved under this commission—nothing has been paid, and I cannot say what they have in hand belonging to the bankrupt—I do not keep the books—I do not know that there is at this moment 20 s. in the pound—I know the debts to be much larger than the amount of the estate.
MR. WIGHTMAN. Q. Did the bankrupt see this book at the last examination? A. yes, decidedly so—it was with the rest, at the official assignees.
COURT. Q. Is it the custom to exhibit all the books? A. Yes—all the books are generally brought—all the prisoner's books were not produced, because some were very old ones.
JANE SCAFE . I live in Whitby, in Yorkshire. My brother was a clerk at the shop of Mr. William Rotherham—I think I know his handwriting—this account of mine, in this book, is in my brother's writing—he was, for anything I know, the chief clerk, and manager at Mr. Rotherham's; and, I believe, he kept his books—I gave an acceptance for a bill of 123 l., about the end of October, 1834—I did not pay 50 l. on the 16th of May, 1834; or 50 l. on the 2nd of June, 1834; nor did I afterwards pay 53 l. 3 s —the bill which I accepted was paid by my brother; but I did not see it paid—it was in Yorkshire.
JAMES WILLIAM BUCKLAND . I am a refiner of metals, and a manufacturer of British plate. I know the bankrupt—I recollect his coming to me sometime after his last examination—somewhere early in the year, but I cannot tell the time—it was soon after he became a bankrupt—I had an interview, (but not at our house,) after he past his last examination—it was at No. 18, Finsbury-square, at Mr. John Scafe's, who had been his clerk—I learnt from the bankrupt that he had passed his examination—he said he was asked at the court whether he had not a valuable gold watch—he said he had a watch in his pocket: and drew it out of his pocket, and put it down, and said, "There is my valuable gold watch;" and there was some observation which I cannot recollect; but he was told, if that was the watch to put it into his pocket—the watch which he produced was a metal one with a bit of string—he showed me the watch he produced before the Commissioners—it was a metal watch—I saw it with my own eyes—he said nothing but Miss. Scafe's debt; but sometimes afterwards I had occasion to go to Holloway to inquire for Mr. William Rotherham—I went, but could not find him—this was, I think, the beginning of August—I returned to Clifton-street, and waited till I saw him—I do not recollect whether he said any thing about re-taking his old premises—the conversation was in consequence of a Bill of Exchange which he had put into circulation—he had a second to take up the first, and he put them
both into circulation—I saw him about this bill on which I was sued—he said he could not do any thing, for that he had saved but 400 l. or 500 l., and that was every farthing expended; and he had been obliged to pledge his watch that morning to buy a dinner at home for his family—he said he came to Clifton-street to see Mr. Scafe, who had a bill of about £150, which was drawn to Scafe's sister; and likewise there was 30 l. in addition to the bill due from Miss. Scafe, and 20 l. from Mr. Anderson, making altogether 200 l.; and if Mr. Scafe would give him the bill he would make him a present of the remainder of the money, which was about 50 l.—he said so—he said about 30 l. was due from Miss. Scafe, in addition to the bill; and that Scafe held a bill drawn on his sister.
Cross-examined by MR. LEE. Q. I believe you are some relation to Scafe? A. yes; we married two sister—no one was present at this conversation—Scafe was not present at that conservation—I am not on bad terms with the bankrupt, nor on good terms—I have been a bankrupt once, which was about September; and I was insolvent once, about twenty years ago—I gave one bill for £195, and I gave a second to make up the first—the first was an accommodation bill, and the second was given to take up the first, which irritated me very much at first—I did not say I would take the first opportunity to make him recollect it—I had the opportunity to do it if I wished—I gave the account I have here when I was subpoenaed by Mr. parker, about a week ago—I mentioned it at my own house, to Mr. Brickley (solicitor) about six months since—I did not send information to Mr. Parker—I told Mr. Brickley, because he was a friend—I do not know where he lives—I had known him twenty years—he is to be found at Fenchurch-street—he was not security for me in any transaction—Mr. Parker lives, I believe, in St. Paul's Church-yard—when I applied for my bills, the prisoner said he had nor a shilling in the world, nor a bit of bread to put into the months of his children—he said he had pledged a watch—that was in the beginning of August.
MR. LEE to MR. BELCHER. Q. Will you tell me the amount of assests? A. His stock sold for about 6, 000 l —his goods debts were 1400 l— —the amount received by me in all, to the 15th of October, was 8, 125 l. 14 s. 10 d.—I do not know the amount of debts proved altogether—the bankrupt made a great mistake, and it was dispute between him and me—it was stated that nearly 2, 000 l. had been expended in white-washing, painting, and glazing, and repairing the premises, over and over again, but I had them valved—the officer took the stock at 9, 669 l. odd, and it was valued at 3, 000 l. more.
MARY WRIGHTSON . I am the sister of Mrs. William Rotherham. I recollect Mr. William Rotherham becoming a bankrupt—I received a parcel from Mrs. William Rotherham after the bankrupt of Mr. William Rotherham—I cannot tell how soon after—I cannot tell where it was—I was living with my father then—Mr. William Rotherham became a bankrupt in January last years—it might be longer than a month after, that I received the parcel—Mrs. Rotherham opened it—the prisoner was not present.
(The prisoner read a long address, entering into the particular of his embarrasment, and stating, that tit his affairs had been managed judiciously, there would have been an ample surplus after the payment of his debts in
full; and that he had no idea of defrauding his creditors, who had been in advised in their proceedings against him.)
NOT GUILTY .
NEW COURT, Tuesday, February 9 th, 1836.
Fifth Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18360201-563a]
563. DANIEL FOOT was indicated for stealing, on the 30th of January, 2 rings, value 31 s., the goods of John Foote, his master.
JOHN FOOTE, JUN . I am a surgeon, and live in Tavistock-street, Covent-garden. The prisoner came into my service as errand boy, on Thursday, the 28th of January, at 5 s. a week—I took two rings from my finger that evening, and placed them in a drawer—I missed them on Sunday morning—the prisoner had absconded at that time—on the Monday morning I took him, and then he asked if I would let him off it he returned them both—he is no relation to me.
WILLIAM BOURN . I am shopman to Mr. Lamp, a pawnbroker, in Stanhope-street. I produce the ring which I took in from a young man, on Saturday the 31st of January—it was not the prisoner.
JOHN KIRKMAN , I am a policeman. I took the prisoner into custody—he denied all knowledge of the rings—when going to the station-house he said he might as well tell all about it—he took the rings, but did it through distress—I asked what he did with them—he said he had given one to a boy, who he knew very well, to pawn; and the other to a boy who ran away with it.
(Sarah Harding and Richard Wright gave the prisoner a good character.)
GUILTY. Aged 15.—Recommended to mercy. — Confined Nine Days.
[trialAccount t18360201-564a]
564. BENJAMIN WALLER was indicated for stealing, on the 3rd of February, 180 stay-laces, value 3 l. 10 s., the good of John Henry Machu his master.
MR. PAYNE conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN NELSON . I have been six months in Mr. Machu's employ. The prisoner was also in his employ, and slept in the warehouse, last Wednesday night, about half-past nine o'clock, I came into the warehouse—I had been out for some beer—when I came in, I saw the prisoner in the corner of the warehouse, by the silk laces—he had got the silk laces in his right hand, putting them under a wrapper—there were boxes at his right hand, putting them under a wrapper—there were boxes at his right hand which contained the laces—they were French white laces in the top box, and underneath they were silk-there was a white paper hanging out of the box—I did not say anything to him—we went and sat down by the fire, and drank out beer—about twenty minutes after that, as I was reading, he left me, and went to the staircase, took the wrapper and put it into the gas-meter cupboard—he than came and sat by the fire again—I said nothing to him till the clerk came, at about a quarter past ten o'clock—I had previously made a communication to the clerk and when he came in, I winked at him to send the prisoner away, which he did, and while he was gone—Mr. Machu afterwards came in with a policeman, and said, there was an alarm of thieves in the places, and asked the prisoner if all was right—he said it was, that he had been round the place, and there was
nothing the matter—he changed very white—my asked him how the laces came in the cupboard—he made no answer.
THOMAS HOLLOWAY . I am clerk to Mr. Machu. In consequence of a communication made to me by Nelson, I sent the prisoner out—I have heard Nelson give his evidence—it is corrcet.
COURT. Q. Why did you send the prisoner out? A. Nelson did not know how to act exactly, and he explained that to me.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. How long has he been in your master's service? A. Twelve months—Nelson spoke to me the morning before about this
JOHN HENRY MACHU . I am a silk-manufacturer, and live in Twisters-alley, Bunhill-row, St. Luke's I have heard the statement made by Nelson—it is correct—I said, "Every thing is not right here, there is some thieving going on," or something to that effect—the prisoner, with others, was sitting round the fire at the time—I took a candle, and went towards the gas-meter cupboard—I said, "Come here this moment"—he did not move—I said, "Ben, came here"—he then did not move—I said again, "Come here"—he then came, and I said, "Pull every thing out of this place, "Which he did, but I thought rather reluctantly—I said, "Come, every thing out" and he pulled out with ropes and dirty paper that was there, a wrapper containing the silk laces—I said, "How came they here?"—he made no answer—I said, "You rascal, you put them there"—he still made no answer, but appeared very much confused, and I saw a tear dropping down his face, and when I went in he turned exceedingly pale, and put up his arms—the value of the laces is 3 l. 10 s.
Cross-examined. Q. Is that the wholesale price. A. Yes—there are fifteen dozen of them—I have no partner in this business—I do not wish to press the charge in any way—he had half-a-guinea a week and a penny a night for his beerhe is not married—he slept in the warehouse for security.
(George Draper, of Bamborough-court, St. Luke's, gave the prisoner good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 18— Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18360201-565]
565. JAMES STEVENSON was indicted for stealing, on the 29th of January, 1 looking-glass and frame, value 4 l., the goods of William West.
WILLIAM WEST . I am a broker, and live in West Cottages, Cambridge-health-road. I bought a looking-glass, which I placed where I purchased it—I saw it safe about ten minutes after one o'clock, on the 29th of January—I missed it afterwards—( produced) this is it—I Knew it to be the glass when I was at worship-street; but it is so mutilated and broken all to pieces since—the policeman had an accident with it—I Have every reason to think it is the same—that I saw at Wroship-street was the same.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Who did you buy this furniture of? A. Mr. Wilson, a spirit-merchant—he was confined in the Fleet-prison at the time I bought it—the prisoner was one of the men I employed in removing the things—Mrs. Wilson was in the house when I got there—there was a padlock and chain on the front gate of the house—I did not break that with a crow-bar.
Q. On your oath, did you not tell the prisoner to take the glass to his lodging and keep it till he should see you, as you wanted to dispose of it privately? A. On my oath, no such passed—I conceive Mr. Will.
son had a right to dispose of the furniture, his rent was all paid—I do not think the Sheriff had been in—if he had, he would have prevented my taking the things away and purchasing them—I have never paid for the glass—there was no receipt given, or any instrument drawn up—only a verbal agreement—this was not purchased by auction—the things were not all packed up ready to be take away when I got there—my men took them down, and the glass too—I do not think there was any article, packed up.—I had made the agreement with Wilson some days before, because I had been receiving some furniture perparatory to this—there was no person present at the agreement.
COURT. Q. You employed this man to take the property, did you? A. Yes; as to the removal of the goods—at noon-day—help me to put them into a van—I know nothing more than than seeing the glass on the lawn—I did not see the prisoner take it away—I did not make the bargain at the Fleet, but at the house, prepartory to my seeing him there.
MR. PAYNE. Q. Have you ever been in any other part of this Court? A. Yes; that is not relevant to this question—I have been at Clerkenwell—I indicted a man named Hall, who robbed me of 5 l —I was never indicted for having sworn falsely.
Q. On your oath, did not ten witnesses swear on that occasion, that they would not believe you on your oath, and the man was acquitted? A. I dare say they could have produced fifty, they were such a set of man—persons came forward to say, and he was acquitted certainly—I was never in Chelmsford gaol, nor any prison in Essex—the prisoner was not employed to take the glass home; but only to get the things into the van.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18360201-566]
566. CHARLES COOKE was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of January, 1 copper, value 15 s., the goods of Ann Nash, and fixed to a building against, the statute, &c; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
MARIA NEWTON . I am servant to Miss Ann Nash, of Camden-tow, Kentish Town. She had a copper at her house, fixed to a building—it was lost on the 6th of January—this is it.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. How do you know it? A. By the burn; there was water in it when it when it was stolen—I can swear to it by the burn at the side, and grease inside—I saw it between eleven and twelve o'clock in the wash-house—I did not miss it till the policeman came in the evening—the wash-house is at the bottom of the garden.
ALEXANDER LYON ( police-constable S 43.) I was son duty in Camden-town, and saw the prisoner with a copper on the evening of the 6th of January—he went to Mr. Bignell's—I heard him ask Mr. Bignell to buy the copper—I came in, and asked were be got it from—he said, "From Mr. Sherriff of Kentish-town"—I said I was not satisfied, and he must go with me there; and when we got outside the door, he said he had stolen it.
Cross-examined. Q. Had you said he had better tell you all about it? A. No—I left the copper at the shop—I got back again about seven o'clock—I know it was the same I had left, by my initials, which I had put on it—the prisoner said he had stolen it when he came out of the shop—he did not tell me where from.
GEORGE WILLIAM MURRAY . I am a policeman. I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, from the office of the clerk of the Court ( read) the prisoner is the man who was tried.
Prisoner. The Policeman has been telling falsehoods.
(William Carroll, a painter and glazier, of Kentish-town Benjamin Swain, a tinman, of Kentish-town; and Edward Gibley, a slater, of Somers'-town; gave the prisoner a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 21.— Transported for Seven Years.

568. WILLIAM BLANDFORD was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of January, 1 book-stand, value 8 s.; 6 pieces of velvet, containing 4 1/4 value 5 s.; part of a book-stand, value 13 d.; 1 pen-rack, value 2 s.; 4 looking-glasses and frames, value 8 s.; 1 watch-stand, value 4 s.; 1 box, value 1 s.; 2 dresses and frames, value 1 l. 12 s. 2 glass bottles, value 1 s. 6 d.; 3 boxes, value 12 s.; and 1 inkstand, value 2 s.; the goods of Charles Thomas Pratt, his master.
CHARLES THOMAS PRATT . I am a cabinet-maker, and live in Vineyardwalk, Coppice-row, Clerkenwell. The prisoner was my journeyman for fourteen months—I missed some things and gave him into custody—he said he lived at No. 10, Bowling-green-lane—I went there and found part of the property and some duplicates, on the 15th, I found this velvet.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Is there any mark on that velvet? A. I have a piece here which this was torn from—this is my box—I cannot swear to this in particular—I had a dozen of them made, and filled them up as dressing-cases—the duplicate of it was found at the Prisoner's lodging—he was constantly in my employ for fourteen months, and had 25 s. a week—I believe he has a wife and two children—he has borrowed money of me—I lent him 2 l. when he first came into my employ, and he repaid me a few shillings at a time—he did not pay me 2 l. 10 s. for it—I cannot exactly say how much it was—he asked me some time ago to lean him 2 l., for which he accounted to me, I think, 2 l. 4 s., or 5 s.—the loan was for a month or two—I did not charge him 2 s. for the loan of 10 s. to my recollection I will not swear it—you will understand I never charged him any thing—it was his own offer—he often stopped away from work longer than he should, and I considered he was only paying back my own money.
HENRY ASTELL . I am a pawnbroker. I produce the dressing case, which was Pawned by a man, nine months ago, in the name of John Brown—I cannot swear to the prisoner.
JAMES JONES . I am a pawnbroker. I produce this box—I believe it was pledged by the prisoner, but I cannot swear it—this glass was pledged by a female.
JOSEPH SMALLEY . I am a policeman. I took the prisoner—he told me where he lodge—I went with him, and found these things, and the duplicates of the other property. (Samuel Elliott, a cabinet-maker, of Charterhouse-lane, gave the prisoner a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 28.— Confined Two Months.

569. ELLEN CALLAN was indicted for stealing, on the 22nd of July, two gowns, value 10 s. 1 scarf, value 5 s.; 1 pair of half-boots, value 3 s.; 2 yards of linen cloth, value 2 s.; the goods of Ruth Weighell, now the wife of John Byfield, and that she had been before convicted of felony.
RUTH BYFIELD . I am the wife of John Byfield, a bootmaker, in vine-street, In July last was a window, and my name was Ruth Weighell—the prisoner was a servant at the London Hotel, in Albermarle-street—at that time I mangle for that house—I missed the property stated—the prisoner came to my house while I was out for a day's work—I found my box broken open, and the property gone—I took them out of pawn myself.
( Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner. There were other people in the house besides me.
ANN PRINCE . I am the wife of Richard prince, and live in Great St. Andrew-street, Seven Dials. I know the Prisoner—in July last the came to me, in Sidney's-alley, and asked me if I knew of a pawnbrokers's—I went with her to a pawnbroker's, and she pawned these articles—when I came out she asked me if I would accept of the tickets—I did not want them—she said she should never take them out, that the gowns wee too small for her, and the boots pinched her—unfortunately I took the ticket—I gave it to Maroney, who went and took the two gowns out for 5 s., and left the remainder in, for 5 s.—went in a fortnight to get things, and was taken into custody, and taken to Marlborough-street for it.
prisoner. The witness is a perfect stranger to me—about two months ago, I met her in New-street, Covent, but there was no conversstion between us. Witness. It is no such thing.
SAMUEL HASLOCK . I produce this gown, which was pledged by the wit-Maroney.
BRIDGET MARONEY . I pledged these two frocks at Mrs. Haslock's—Mrs. Prince gave me the ticket, and I took them out for 5 s., where they were in for 10 s., at Mr. Lawton's in Green-street—they were pledged in the name of Smith.
ANN PRINCE re-examined. I gave Maroney the ticket when they were in for 10 s., because the gowns would fit her, and not myself—the shopman is not here from Mr. Lawton's, where they were first pledge—it is at the corner of Green-street—I met the prisoner in King-street, Covent-garden.
GEORGE WEIGHELL . I am the prosecutrix's brother. she left me in charge of the house—the prisoner came there that day, and went out with a basket—I went out with some clothes, to take to Jermyn-street, and left
her at home—when I came back I found she was gone, and the key left outside the door—we did not find the robbery out for two or three days—no one else had any opportunity of taking the property, between the time of my coming home and finding it out.
Prisoner. Q. Was not the door left open when you went down into the kitchen—did not go for you, to get you a situation? A. You said you would—I was gone about ten minutes.
THOMAS CLARK . I am a policemen. I got this certificate of the prisoner's former conviction form Mr. Clark's office, ( read)the prisoner is the woman who was tried.
Prisoner. I never took the thinks.
